Trump Is Looking for Diesel in All the Wrong Places
2026-09-15

Trump Is Looking for Diesel in All the Wrong Places

The Atlantic | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
This is an edition of The Atlantic Daily, a newsletter that guides you through the biggest stories of the day, helps you discover new ideas, and recommends the best in culture. Sign up for it here. Gasoline prices have been up all summer, as have jet-fuel prices, as have home-heating-oil prices—but nothing has jumped quite like diesel. The average price for a gallon of diesel in the United States just hit an all-time high of $6.27, almost double what it was in late February. It’s no secret that the Iran war is largely responsible for America’s energy woes. So far, Donald Trump’s strategy has been to downplay these increases as temporary side effects (a “little glitch,” he said in March) and remind everyone that oil prices were higher under Joe Biden (which is true). Lately, though, the president has been telling us to look away from Iran and toward a different war. Over the weekend, he called on Ukraine’s president to stop striking Russian oil refineries, which he said was “causing a shortage of diesel.” And yesterday morning, he announced a partial energy cease-fire between the two countries, claiming that the “World’s Diesel price rise is mostly caused by the Russia/Ukraine War, not Iran.” (Although both Ukraine and Russia have signaled that they’d be open to such an agreement, they continued attacking each other last night.) Focusing on Eastern Europe may be a convenient distraction tactic, but the reality is that this war is ultimately less important for U.S. diesel than the one that Trump is waging in the Middle East. Diesel’s rally has to do with how it’s distilled. Diesel, like gasoline, primarily comes from crude oil, which has been in short supply since the Strait of Hormuz was effectively shut down in February. Middle Eastern crude, which is sludgy and sulphurous, is particularly conducive to producing diesel. Making matters worse, many local refineries have been attacked or have preemptively gone offline, leaving the region less equipped to handle the crude that remains. Yemen’s Houthis attacked a major Saudi facility last week as a prelude to a broader offensive. The refinery problem is global. At their summer peak, the world’s facilities were producing 4.2 million fewer barrels a day than they were last year. As Trump has pointed out, Ukrainian attacks on refineries in Russia—usually the world’s second-largest diesel exporter—have only compounded the issue. With Moscow maintaining its ban on diesel exports (meant to protect domestic supply), more countries are turning to the U.S. to make up the difference. American plants are now “running all-out,” per the American Petroleum Institute, using almost all of their processing power, and the U.S. is exporting more refined-oil products on average than it was last year. U.S. refineries typically shut down for at least a couple of weeks for maintenance around this time of year, too, which could potentially squeeze supply even more. Still, Matt Smith, the director of commodity research at the data firm Kpler, told me that Ukrainian aggression “isn’t equally to blame, let alone the leading factor,” in America’s rising diesel prices. Even if Russia and Ukraine agreed to a cease-fire today, global refining capacity wouldn’t magically return right away. As I wrote in May, damaged facilities take time to repair and come back online once they’ve been shut down. Gregory Brew, an energy historian and a senior analyst at Eurasia Group, told me that for any détente to have an effect on diesel prices, “it would likely need to last several weeks at least.” Meanwhile, the situation in the Middle East remains unstable. The Saudi Arabian government recently shut down a crucial pipeline that was damaged in a strike by Iran-aligned militias in Iraq (the pipeline had been moving 4 to 5 percent of the global oil supply—several million barrels a day). Combine these risks with elevated seasonal demand for diesel, which in the U.S. tends to peak around the harvest season, and it’s clear that the European conflict is only one force among many behind this price spike. Americans are feeling the effects. One gas station in San Diego has been selling diesel for $9.99 a gallon, which is as high as its three-digit physical display will permit. Groceries may become more expensive because farm equipment and refrigerated 18-wheelers tend to run on diesel. Trains predominantly use diesel, too, and railroads’ fuel surcharges on grain shipments have more than doubled over the past year. Nearly 5 million homes could be paying more for heating oil (which is chemically similar to diesel and subject to some of the same economic pressures) as well. As everyday costs continue to climb, voters are growing more and more dissatisfied with Trump’s handling of the economy. And over the past few months, the president has been looking for someone—anyone—to share the blame. A speedy Trump-negotiated end to the Ukraine war is unlikely for many reasons. But even in the fantasy world where he pulls that off, it wouldn’t save him from a diesel spike of his own creation. Here are four new stories from The Atlantic: When I was 13, not long after my father had committed suicide, I asked my mother, “What’s the Oedipus complex?” “Why?” she replied at once. “Are you afraid you’ve got it?” Maybe. I wasn’t aware of wanting to murder my father, but in the four years after he suffered a massive stroke at 43, he’d been a volatile presence in our house, given to bursts of rage and tears, or rendered ominously mute by his paralysis. When he disappeared on December 6, 1972, leaving my mother, my sister, and me, I was troubled by a sense of profound relief. Unable to understand, let alone forgive, myself, I went searching for insight on the shelves of my parents’ narrow, high-walled study. Books offered a way to replace real self-knowledge with an official version—the idea of “guilt” instead of the intolerable feeling itself. In a volume called Oedipus: Myth and Complex, amid thickets of psychoanalytic jargon, I found what I was looking for. Read. Lily Meyer explores Priya Parker’s new book, The Art of Fighting, and how it suggests that arguing isn’t about winning but seems preoccupied with it anyway. Debate. Jon Ronson’s The Castle argues that men are giving up on society because they have nothing better to do, Jeremy Gordon writes. Rafaela Jinich contributed to this newsletter. When you buy a book using a link in this newsletter, we receive a commission. Thank you for supporting The Atlantic.

Congress Might Be About to Hand Trump Sweeping Tariff Powers
2026-09-15

Congress Might Be About to Hand Trump Sweeping Tariff Powers

The Atlantic | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
The Lindsey Graham Sanctioning Russia Act, which the House will likely vote on this week, looks like a no-brainer for supporters of Ukraine who have been calling for President Trump to get tougher on Russia. A large majority of America’s existing sanctions on Russia, which target thousands of government officials and companies, are currently enforced by executive order, meaning that the president can remove them whenever he wants. The Graham bill would turn those sanctions into law: If Trump wanted to end them, he would need Congress’s approval. It also targets a few Russian officials and companies by name, further reducing Trump’s discretion. These provisions help explain why the late Senator Graham, one of Ukraine’s strongest defenders in Congress, originally championed the bill. President Volodymyr Zelensky himself has urged legislators to pass it, as have more than 30 national-security and foreign-policy experts who argue that it’s in Kyiv’s best interest. But the bill is not what it seems. The legislation would impose no significant new sanctions on Russia, and it would allow Trump to waive any of its measures simply by claiming that doing so is in the national interest. In other words, a law that appears to handcuff Trump would offer him a key to free himself. What’s more, its most important provisions are about not sanctions per se, but tariffs. The bill could provide Trump with the same kind of sweeping authority to impose tariffs that the Supreme Court took away from him in February, allowing him to intensify his trade war with many of America’s allies and partners. The legislation would give the president authority to impose levies of up to 100 percent on the five largest importers of Russian crude oil and the five largest importers of Russian natural gas, which include China, India, France, Japan, Hungary, and Slovakia. It also allows him to impose tariffs on the five countries that are deemed to be the biggest facilitators of Russia’s evasion of oil sanctions—a vague provision largely subject to presidential discretion. (There are a few carve-outs for the natural-gas category, but not for the others.) Peter Harrell, a trade lawyer who served in the Biden administration, told me that the bill “gives Trump much more flexibility on tariffs than traditional tariff laws. There are no guardrails or restrictions.” The simplest reading of the legislation suggests that Trump can impose tariffs on only the countries that appear in the various top-five lists. But Harrell noted that Trump’s second-term track record suggests that he might try to interpret the law more expansively, perhaps by treating the European Union as a single country and imposing tariffs on the entire bloc all at once. Harrell also warned that the administration might use the law as a pretext to impose tariffs on countries that have little or nothing to do with Russia evading sanctions. It wouldn’t be the first time that Trump has employed such a pretext. Consider his 25 percent tariff on India, which the Supreme Court has since struck down. The levy was ostensibly meant to punish the country for importing oil from Russia. But reporting suggests that the decision had more to do with Trump’s annoyance that Prime Minister Narendra Modi didn’t give him credit for the cease-fire that India and Pakistan signed last year. Going beyond the letter of the Graham bill could well result in litigation for the administration. But Trump would still have ample reason to take an aggressive approach. For one thing, the legal process takes a long time—most trade lawyers think that the February ruling came at warp speed, even though it was a year in the making. While the case was moving through the courts, they consistently allowed the Trump administration to continue collecting tariff revenue in the meantime. In all likelihood, Trump would be free to use the tariff tool expansively for most of the rest of his term. Trump is evidently less worried about Russia evading sanctions than he is about oil prices. At the start of the Iran war, he waived some sanctions on Russian oil in an attempt to keep prices from going up. There is little reason to think that he will act more forcefully against Russian energy now, because oil prices remain high and the midterms are just a month away. Indeed, he has failed to update and enforce sanctions that are already on the books, despite bipartisan support. Moreover, Trump recently sent Jared Kushner and Steve Witkoff to Moscow to dangle the prospect of sanctions relief in exchange for some modest steps toward deescalating the war. Read: The warrior-witches of Ukraine’s resistance Still, supporters of the bill believe that the benefits outweigh the risks. Eric Ciaramella, a senior fellow at the Carnegie Endowment for International Peace who served on the National Security Council as an adviser on Ukraine policy in the first Trump administration, told me, “If Trump wanted to hurt Europe, he has lots of ways to do that already. This is just a marginal increase in that risk, and it adds some political pressure on him to get tougher on Russia.” Whether such pressure would get Trump to change, however, is another question. Perhaps the strongest argument in favor of the bill is simply that the Ukrainians want it. The legislation would no doubt serve as a symbol of Congress’s support. But Ukraine needs material, not symbolic, support—particularly weapons such as air-defense interceptors and greater economic assistance to survive the winter. Trump has the power to provide both, but he has chosen not to. Turning the Graham bill into law isn’t likely to give him a sudden change of heart. Yesterday, Democrats tried but failed to introduce amendments that would exempt the EU from being treated as a country, tighten the national-security waiver, and scrap the broad allowances for tariffs on countries other than Russia. Without those measures, the bill amounts to little more than a permission slip for Trump to restart his trade war with America’s allies and partners. That’s not a price worth paying just to give Ukraine a symbolic victory.
The Atlantic
Math Can’t Go On Like This
2026-09-15

Math Can’t Go On Like This

The Atlantic | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
In late July, one of the world’s most famous mathematicians issued a warning to his field. Math is entering a “turbulent period,” the UCLA professor Terence Tao said in front of a packed lecture hall at the International Congress of Mathematicians. AI is getting so good at math, he said, that it’s causing a “crisis in our mathematical values and practices.” Earlier in the same week that Tao gave his talk at math’s most prestigious conference, a researcher at Anthropic had used Claude to disprove an 87-year-old math conjecture. Still, when I asked mathematicians at the conference about their experiences with AI, most hadn’t thought deeply about the topic. Many seemed a little surprised that a journalist was asking them about it at all. Now mathematicians are paying attention. Last week, OpenAI announced that its bots had cracked the Navier-Stokes problem, one of the seven Millennium Prize problems regarded as among the grandest challenges in the field. Navier-Stokes has stumped mathematicians since the 1930s: “It’s a career-killing problem—you can spend all your career doing it and never get the solution,” David Dritschel, a mathematician at the University of St. Andrews, told me. OpenAI said its swarm of 10,000 AI agents solved the problem in less than four days. In the math world, the initial shock over OpenAI’s result has morphed into something of an existential reckoning for the discipline. Although OpenAI was able to solve the Navier-Stokes problem only by pulling from nearly a century’s worth of human efforts, academics are realizing just how much power the AI industry now has over math. “We’re seeing how swiftly an entire field can be ravaged by AI,” Max Weinreich, a math professor at CUNY Baruch, told me. OpenAI’s breakthrough is as notable as how it was achieved. The night before OpenAI announced its solution, the NYU mathematician Tristan Buckmaster released a statement alleging that he had been scooped by OpenAI. With the heavy assistance of AI tools, Buckmaster and his collaborator, Levent Alpöge (who works at Anthropic), had made major progress toward solving the Navier-Stokes problem. Buckmaster implied that OpenAI might have copied their approach by accessing his messages with OpenAI’s agents. “Almost nobody else I know of was working on it,” he wrote. In an announcement the next day, OpenAI said that it had tasked its AI systems to work on all of the Millennium Problems after hearing rumors that Anthropic had solved two of them. OpenAI claims that it focused on the Navier-Stokes problem only after finding promising partial results, and that it never looked at Buckmaster’s inputs. In any case, OpenAI poured incredible resources into solving this problem—expending millions of dollars in tokens—all because it heard an unfounded rumor of a solution. (OpenAI declined to comment.) This drama has underlined concerns many mathematicians have about AI developments within the field. AI companies are encouraged to solve problems in a way that generates good headlines for their bots—marshaling resources that academics never could—but those solutions won’t necessarily aid further discoveries or help humans understand mathematics. On Friday, 25 winners of the Fields Medal (sometimes called the Nobel Prize of mathematics) released a statement called “A Severe Misalignment of AI in Mathematics.” “The push by AI companies to solve mathematical problems as a benchmark is detrimental to the science of mathematics, and to the mathematical community,” they wrote. All of this can seem obtuse. Isn’t the point of mathematics to solve problems? But there’s more to a problem than just knowing whether it’s true. Solving a major problem in pure mathematics is often like getting a man on the moon: symbolically meaningful but with little direct value. Even though the Navier-Stokes problem deals with one of the most important models in fluid dynamics—a topic of great interest to airplane designers, climate modelers, and many other scientists—knowing the actual result is basically useless. However, with both a math problem and the moon landing, the process of reaching that goal can indirectly develop all sorts of useful tools applicable to other problems. As part of the Apollo missions, NASA engineers and contractors had to solve subproblems that ultimately proved useful elsewhere. The need for an onboard computer, for example, helped spark the nascent computer-chip industry. Perhaps more surprising, to ensure that astronauts did not get sick while in space, the Apollo program helped set food-safety standards that have since become standard in the industry. Mathematicians hope that OpenAI’s Navier-Stokes proof will end up containing ideas that make it easier to study all sorts of other problems, inside and outside of pure mathematics. This has happened before. For instance, efforts to prove Fermat’s Last Theorem produced mathematical tools that now underpin the newest online encryption methods. It’s still too early to tell what humans might be able to learn from OpenAI’s proof. The paper is 166 pages and “almost incomprehensible,” Dritschel said. Yet over the coming months and years, mathematicians will exert substantial effort to dissect and distill OpenAI’s proof until its central ideas are understood enough to (hopefully) be applied elsewhere. Humans won’t be able to do that for most AI-generated math results, however. Already, there are AI proofs that no human understands, not because the ideas are impossible to grasp, but because no one has had the time to look through the solution carefully. It’s easy to imagine a future in which mathematicians use AI to prove ever more impressive results ever more quickly—but they don’t understand what they’re proving in much detail. AI systems can speed the rate of problem-solving, but they can’t make it that much easier for human mathematicians to grasp a proof. So if the field as we know it is to survive, mathematicians need to figure out how to adapt. One idea is to give less credit for solving a problem and more credit for work that communicates the ideas behind a solution in a way that can benefit the broader mathematical community. Yet it’s hard to say exactly how that works in practice. Unlike solving problems, evaluating how well a mathematician has communicated with their peers has no clear metrics. It will take time for mathematicians to figure out the best response. All the while, AI is continuing to develop incredibly quickly. In less than the time that it takes to complete a Ph.D., systems such as ChatGPT have gone from struggling with basic arithmetic to solving some of the most famous problems in the world. Math’s turbulent period, as Tao deemed it, isn’t ending anytime soon.

I don't know about any particular phone, but in many devices the long press power off is an old-fashioned kill switch that charges a capacitor, eventually reaching the voltage to turn off a transistor connected to a "power good" line on the power management IC. And inside the IC it's the same deal. So as long as the button works, it should be able to hard power off, even if the software is badly bugged. Actually numerous times I've seen people complaining their phone or tablet wouldn't turn off, when actually the software was just frozen and they didn't know about long press power off. "May I see that?" "Wow... that's amazing!" "To you, maybe..." Not necessarily a capacitor; sometimes a little counter circuit, on the MCU but independent of the CPU and not controllable (or vetoable) by it. In either case, not prone to systemic failures of the higher-level logic. People overestimate the difficulty of adding low-level contingencies to high-level systems, and underestimate engineers' eagerness to do so. But this is the sort of thing that EEs worry about all the time. When I'm worried my phone doesn't turn itself off, I just use a small flyswatter. (But it's expensive! Despite the Supreme Court’s Ruling, Trump’s Still Trying to Tip the Midterms
2026-09-15

Despite the Supreme Court’s Ruling, Trump’s Still Trying to Tip the Midterms

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. On Monday, the Supreme Court blocked Donald Trump’s effort to throw mail balloting into chaos ahead of the November midterm elections. But that was just one prong of Trump’s attempts to tilt races toward Republicans. Today we’ll explain the others. This Supreme Court ruling seems like a big deal. What exactly happened? You aren’t wrong. As the legal expert Richard Hasen explains in Slate, Trump’s executive order would have created onerous new requirements for mail-in voting that risked disenfranchising millions of elderly, military, and other voters. Seven of nine justices apparently saw that potential catastrophe coming and rejected it, prompting a Tuesday-morning tantrum from the president. Trump has repeatedly tried to get the court to take his side on mail-in ballots, but this ruling looks like the end of the line—at least for this year’s elections. OK, bullet dodged. But what else is Trump doing that we should worry about? Trump’s order was backed by his unfounded claims that mail voting is fraudulent, part of his broader obsession with the false notion that Democrats are “cheating” to win elections. Trump has said he wants to “take over” elections, which are currently administered by state-level officials. That decentralized system is actually a bulwark against the kind of cheating Trump claims to care about—and against a sore-loser president trying to change election rules to protect his own interests. Still, Trump’s administration is trying to get states to hand over voter data and hunting through the rolls for alleged fraud. It’s true that individuals have sometimes illegally cast ballots. But many of those cases have been accidents, like when a software error in New Jersey improperly registered noncitizens in 2023 and 2024, and widespread fraud is a myth. Per the Heritage Foundation, a conservative think tank that helped write Project 2025, there have been about 100 cases of noncitizen voting over the past 25 years. Nevertheless, the administration has asked almost every state to turn over databases that include voters’ personal information. Most have resisted, sparking court battles that the administration has overwhelmingly lost. The Department of Homeland Security, meanwhile, has assigned hundreds of employees to parse public voting databases for noncitizens. Some have allegedly posed as voters, potentially violating state privacy laws. Yet Secretary Markwayne Mullin has publicly touted unverified numbers of alleged noncitizen voters and threatened election officials with prison time. But surely those efforts can be contested in court? Democrats and voting-rights groups have already sued over some of Trump’s moves (hence the case rejecting his mail ballot order). But some fear the administration is looking for ways to circumvent courts. Late last month, a whistleblower alleged that the U.S. Postal Service had continued to build an online portal that could’ve thrown out mail ballots en masse despite an order blocking work on it. (The Postal Service claims it hasn’t defied any court orders.) Courts can’t stop Trump from sowing doubt about the soundness of American elections. He has ordered investigations into debunked voting irregularities in states he lost in 2020 and continues to attack voting machines despite there being no evidence that they’ve ever changed election results. That could lay the groundwork to claim there was fraud if certain races don’t go Republicans’ way this fall. And even if judges toss those claims, they may still depress faith in the results. One recent survey found that less than half of Americans expect the midterms to be free and fair, with most Republicans doubting that mailed ballots are counted accurately and most Democrats fearful that Trump will deploy immigration agents to polling places. So, while the voting could ultimately go off without a hitch, the crisis of confidence continues. The midterms are still several weeks away, so let’s turn to a more urgent question: How to enjoy the rest of your evening. My colleagues have some ideas that don’t require an appeal to the Supreme Court. Take a sip: Martinis are savored for their elegant simplicity and refined romanticism. Now their iconic imagery has been repurposed as chintzy merch and wine mom–style decor. Courtney Iseman asks, “When did we as a society collectively decide, We martini, therefore we are?” Reconsider your “smart” home: Cameras are becoming more and more common in home appliances, including in smart ovens, refrigerators, doorbells, and toothbrushes—even as people decry surveillance measures in public. What does this trend mean for our consumption? Steffi Cao has a prediction. Give your brain a workout: What’s an 11-letter “Enya hit with the repeated lyric ‘Sail away, sail away, sail away’ ”? What’s a nine-letter “cutesy term for spooky snakes”? Sharpen your wordplay skills with today’s crossword. Celebrate with Slate: In honor of our 30th birthday, Slate has revived our original 1996 homepage and filled it with some of our favorite stories. Revisit articles by Slate alums Daniel Engber, David Plotz, Emily Yoffe, and more. We’ll be updating this page over the next month, so keep an eye out for more pieces from the vault! Slate was one of the first news outlets I read regularly, so I’m really enjoying the birthday throwback. Happy trails back to 1996, and hope you have a great night! Trumpworld Has Found Its New Jeffrey Epstein
2026-09-15

Trumpworld Has Found Its New Jeffrey Epstein

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. The specter of Jeffrey Epstein continues to haunt the Trump administration seven years after the sexual predator’s death. But the makings of the next Epstein-style scandal—one that ultimately could be even more damaging for Trump and MAGAworld—appears to be unfolding right now. The Tate brothers, Andrew and Tristan, are alleged to be sex predators on a level approaching Epstein and with perhaps even more political clout with Donald Trump. For those who are blessedly unfamiliar with the Tates, they are highly popular “manosphere” influencers who have garnered an international following, particularly among young men, for their particular brand of content focused on their prideful misogyny and flashing of their apparent wealth. They are also accused of running a decadelong international sex-trafficking ring, with credible allegations against them in multiple countries from dozens of accusers involving gruesome acts of sexual violence, abuse, and exploitation against women and minors. And they have many connections to the world’s top political elite, including Donald Trump’s family members and close advisers. Sound familiar? Just like Epstein, the Tates have seemingly leveraged these connections to receive favorable treatment and avoid the full extent of justice. After years of investigations and attempted prosecutions, the Tates find themselves in the crosshairs yet again. In July, they were charged in Britain on rape charges that also included allegations of possession of child sexual abuse imagery—and are currently sitting in a jail in Miami, fighting extradition. And earlier this month, Romanian prosecutors indicted Andrew for trafficking minors, money laundering, sex with a minor, and witness tampering, and Tristan with complicity. But, in a few important ways, the involvement of Trump’s administration and allies with the Tates could be even more egregious than the Epstein controversy. While the Epstein scandal has centered on holding bad actors accountable for past actions and current cover-ups, the Tate brothers’ scandal is a live issue, and there is reason to believe that if the Trump administration buckles in their favor, the brothers could go back to their criminal activities. Unlike Epstein, who generally lurked in the shadows of elite circles, the Tates brazenly and publicly broadcast their depraved behavior for years. The Tates established their popularity by building online and porn businesses, and becoming internet sensations through their aggressive and controversial misogynistic opinions. Indicia of their alleged criminality have been publicly available for years, including Andrew attacking a woman on camera in 2016. In the process, they lured in young women and allegedly sexually abused and raped them. The victims allegedly include minors. Accusations and law enforcement investigations of rape and strangulation have been swirling around the Tates since at least 2015, when they relocated from the U.K. to Romania to escape possible arrest. Rape allegations persisted in the subsequent years, as the Tates continued to grow their online following. In 2023, the Tates and several co-conspirators were formally charged by Romanian authorities, with allegations from about 35 alleged victims, including minors. The Tate brothers were barred from leaving Romania while the prosecution continued, having been labeled flight risks and public dangers. (The Tate brothers deny all allegations of criminal conduct.) Despite this, their following grew. And, just as Epstein used his wealth and social status to develop political relationships, the Tate brothers’ rise in popularity has come with an increase in political influence, which they have eagerly flaunted. The connections between the Tates and Trumpworld multiplied over time, even as accusations against them piled up. They developed a relationship with the president’s youngest son, Barron, who encouraged his father to engage with the manosphere podcasts and ecosystem. They were personally in touch with Donald Trump Jr., who livestreamed with Andrew in 2024 and called the Tates’ Romanian detention in 2023 “absolute insanity.” (The White House has not responded to questions about Barron’s relationship with the Tates.) After the 2024 election, shows of support from top Trump advisers became even louder. Vice President–elect J.D. Vance followed the Tates on X; right before Trump’s inauguration, incoming counselor to the president Alina Habba said she was a “big fan” of the Tates, compared their legal issues to Trump’s, and told Andrew, “If you’re in the States, I got your back.” After Trump took office, one of the Tates’ former lawyers, Paul Ingrassia, became the White House’s liaison to the Justice Department and then the Department of Homeland Security. These connections appeared to pay off almost immediately after Trump’s inauguration. In February 2025, Richard Grenell, the Trump administration’s special envoy, attended the Munich Security Conference with Vice President Vance and seemed to push for the Tates’ release. Vance’s speech to the conference, in part, echoed right-wing critiques of a recent Romanian judicial decision. Meanwhile, Grenell, after being lobbied by the Tates’ PR team to help the brothers, held a side meeting with Romanian officials at the conference in which he reportedly asked how “the Tate boys” were doing. The New Yorker reported that it seemed clear to the Romanian foreign minister that the Americans were supporting the Tates because the Tates backed Trump in 2024, and that the Tates were “playing a role within the bilateral relations” between the two countries. (The White House denied this, and Grenell denied helping the Tates, but has publicly stated: “I support the Tate brothers.”) Nevertheless, Grenell’s and Vance’s behavior appears to have been interpreted as subtle but clear pressure from the Trump administration to release the Tates. Which is precisely what happened days later. Romania lifted the travel restrictions, in the face of fierce opposition by prosecutors, and the brothers gallivanted to meet their MAGA friends in the United States. When they arrived in Miami, Trump’s oldest living political svengali Roger Stone posted a picture with them and wrote: “Richard Grenell secured the release of the Tates.” According to the New Yorker, Andrew “denied that Donald Trump had personally intervened, but said he had learned from powerful people in Bucharest that the restrictions were removed because of American pressure.” With the Tates now finding themselves in law enforcement’s dragnet yet again, the parallels with Epstein are striking. Although the Department of Justice arrested the Tates in July 2026, assisting the U.K.’s extradition efforts under the new criminal charges, and the White House said they wouldn’t get involved in the matter, the brothers are continuing to try to use their influence to pressure the Trump administration. Following the arrest, the Tates’ lawyer said: “Andrew Tate was a material participant in ensuring President Trump’s victory. And if Trump doesn’t support him, and if the next people who are going to be running for office potentially are just like, yeah, down the river he goes, that young cohort of people will break with the Republican Party in the next election.” As the Epstein scandal has taught us, the fact that Trump’s DOJ arrested the Tates does not lessen the severity of what has already transpired, or the risks going forward. The last time the Tates wriggled away from custody and came to the U.S., a woman in California accused Andrew of choking her and battering her head and face; after their encounter, she was diagnosed with post-concussion syndrome. L.A. prosecutors did not ultimately bring charges but she is pursuing a lawsuit against him, including as evidence her medical report and texts in which he threatened to beat her. The same dynamic transpired with Epstein two decades ago: He, too, was arrested and charged but continued to put significant pressure on the government and ultimately received a sweetheart deal. He then went back to his life of luxury and sex crimes. We’ve already seen the Trump administration take aggressive steps to help supporters who have been charged with crimes and convicted, so his administration’s possible involvement in the Tate brothers’ story isn’t necessarily over. The Tates are continuing to challenge their extradition, and ultimately, once they exhaust their legal options, whether they are extradited to face their charges or not would ultimately be up to the Trump administration. The scope of Epstein’s criminal behavior, and the full extent of his social network, was largely ignored while he was at large. Today, the Tates and the apparent depths of their interlocking connections to people around Trump have broadly fallen under the radar of mainstream media coverage. But for younger Americans they are totemic figures in this political and cultural moment, and their influence is particularly pernicious. That’s why there must be full transparency and congressional investigations to unravel what has already happened and expose the full extent of their relationship with Trumpworld, and also to uncover what, if any, efforts are continuing to be made now by their allies to pressure the Trump administration as the Tates continue to face charges in the United Kingdom and Romania. As a member of the legal team representing the victims of Jeffrey Epstein in litigation, I can attest that the struggle to achieve justice after his death has been righteous but hard. The Tates must be held accountable for their actions and neither favor nor fame can be allowed to shield them. The Epstein scandal and what happened to his victims is heinous; the Trump administration had begun to repeat that miscarriage of justice. Hopefully, they won’t be allowed to continue. The Supreme Court’s Conservatives and Liberals Just Came to the Brink—and Finally Said No
2026-09-15

The Supreme Court’s Conservatives and Liberals Just Came to the Brink—and Finally Said No

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. There are two ways of looking at the Supreme Court’s three-sentence ruling in USPS v. California Monday evening, which put an end to Donald Trump’s attempt to disenfranchise millions of elderly, disabled, military, rural, Native American, and other voters in the 2026 midterm elections who depend upon vote by mail to cast a ballot. The first is to lament that the government pushed this case to its limits—going not once, not twice, but three times to the high court—and that two justices, Samuel Alito and Clarence Thomas, were willing to simply ignore mass disenfranchisement and side with Trump’s bogus voter-fraud crusade. The second is to note that the court’s conservatives and liberals came to the brink, saw that ruling the other way risked serious election subversion, and said no. For the time being, let’s celebrate that the center of the court did hold, even as dangers remain ahead in both 2026 and 2028. Slate readers are already familiar with this long saga, so just a brief review is necessary. Back in March, Trump put out an executive order on voting (his second since returning to office in 2025) that, among other things, directed the Postal Service to establish a rule imposing a new set of requirements for states that aimed to continue having their ballots delivered by mail. The rule, issued in late August, mandated that states receive USPS approval for new envelope designs with intelligent bar codes on them, print those bar codes on each envelope, upload a list of their voters to a USPS portal, and submit the ballots; the agency would then check the names encoded in the bar codes against those in the states’ voter lists. The rule further stipulated that USPS should reject entire batches of ballots if there was one potential mismatch. During the brief time when courts had not yet blocked the rule from taking effect, USPS rejected the ballot design of Leon County, Florida, because its required printed notation about return mail was 0.014 of an inch too close to other information on the envelope. Now, complying with all these new regulations would be pretty challenging for election administrators to do, even with lead time and practice. But the government tried to impose the rules for ongoing voting. Envelopes have already been loaded with ballots and addressed. Indeed, North Carolina began mailing ballots on Sept. 4, and other states are doing so now. Even as of Monday’s SCOTUS ruling, USPS did not have a working portal for states to upload their voter lists, not even on a voluntary basis, something the agency had said would be possible by last week. The upshot of USPS’s administrative failure is that the rule would have stopped the agency from accepting ballots from all states because there could be no matching between the envelope and the portal. That means that, across the country, the most vulnerable voters who depend on vote by mail would have been disenfranchised, including core Republican constituencies. The federal district court issued a preliminary injunction partly on this basis, noting the burdens on election administrators and the public interest costs. The court also found that the rule exceeded USPS’s authority to issue. The Supreme Court’s terse order agreed on both points. It held that the government was likely to lose on the legal question of USPS’s authority. And it held that the “equitable factors”—that is, the burden on election administrators and the voting public—outweighed the government’s purported interest in stopping voter fraud. White-hot fury that the government tried so hard to get this rule in place for the 2026 elections, knowing full well the consequences had it been successful at the Supreme Court, is the correct emotion. A good legal brief takes on the strongest argument of the other side and deals with it head-on. Here, the government had to overcome the district court’s factual findings about the unfairness of the rule. But the government’s brief simply ignored the mass disenfranchisement point, one flagged by the states and by plaintiffs from voting rights organizations, as well as in an amicus brief written by professors, a document that the Safeguarding Democracy Project filed in this case. The failure to address the mass disenfranchisement point essentially conceded it, and that was enough for the court majority. But it was not enough for Alito, dissenting along with Thomas. Alito first concluded that the U.S. was likely to win on the merits. When he turned to the equities, he parroted the empty and unsupported claim that the USPS guidelines would stop fraud. On the other side of the ledger, he asserted that states were divided on the equities because some Trump-supporting state attorneys general had filed a brief siding with the government; never mind that some of those states’ chief election officers filed on the other side. In any event, he went on to blame the states for litigating the case so hard that it somehow stopped the government from getting its rule and portal up and running; he also, once again, implicitly blamed Justice Ketanji Brown Jackson for slow-walking the case when it first came before the justices in July. Totally absent from Alito’s balancing were the voters. They weren’t an afterthought; their interests were not considered at all. This is unsurprising, considering that the justice has done more to hurt the cause of voting and voting rights than anyone else on the court in at least a century. So there are good reasons to be furious and worried about what this portends. But let’s not be all doom and gloom. We know that Justice Brett Kavanaugh, who agreed, in a separate concurrence, that the government might win on the merits, saw that the equities favored the voters this time around. And we can be sure that at least two of the court’s other conservatives agreed too, along with the court’s three liberals, on both the merits and the equities. A third conservative justice either agreed with the majority or did not see fit to side with Alito’s dissent or Kavanaugh’s concurrence. That ain’t nothing. Just as in the period after the 2020 presidential election, when Trump tried to subvert the outcome with bogus legal arguments and conspiracy theories, the Supreme Court again stood firm and said no. Back then, it rejected without comment an original petition from Texas AG Ken Paxton to throw out the results from some states that had voted for President Joe Biden. Now it has failed to countenance a mass disenfranchisement just weeks before a critical midterm. The Supreme Court has been terrible on voting rights, but so far it has held the line on actual election rigging. Let’s celebrate that, even as we gird for more battles to come. Trump Wants to Prevent Future Presidents From Fighting Climate Change
2026-09-14

Trump Wants to Prevent Future Presidents From Fighting Climate Change

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. It’s a great day for people who hate the idea of a livable planet. For everyone else, not so much. The Trump administration today plans to both rescind existing rules on climate pollution from power plants and undercut future administrations’ ability to regulate those emissions. These moves face court challenges. But if they go through, they would gut what’s left of U.S. climate policy and undermine efforts to stave off the worst effects of climate change. What do the new Trump rules actually say? Both the Obama and Biden administrations used the Clean Air Act to try to reduce greenhouse gas emissions, which drive climate change. Now Donald Trump, who has labeled climate change a “con job” and twice pulled out of international agreements meant to mitigate it, is reversing those efforts. One of his new rules would reportedly eliminate Biden-era regulations that required power plants that run on coal and natural gas to limit their carbon emissions. If that rule matches a version reporters obtained last year, it will argue that power plants don’t contribute significantly to pollution or climate change because they’re “a small and decreasing part of global emissions,” even though coal remains the worst-polluting fossil fuel. The second proposal would be even more far-reaching. It’s expected to claim that power plant emissions in general don’t meet the Clean Air Act’s standard for Environmental Protection Agency regulation. The power industry is, behind transportation, the second-largest source of planet-warming emissions in the United States and the second-largest producer of greenhouse gas emissions in the world. Kneecapping the EPA’s ability to regulate the industry under the Clean Air Act would gut one of the main ways Democratic presidents have tried to minimize climate impacts and tie future Democratic presidents’ hands. What else is Trump doing to fuel climate change? Earlier this year, Trump rescinded a long-standing EPA finding that greenhouse gases jeopardize human health. That determination, known as the “endangerment finding,” had been the basis of federal climate regulation for nearly two decades. Without it, the administration has effectively given itself carte blanche to go after all manner of climate regulations. Trump has lifted restrictions on vehicle tailpipe emissions, canceled billions for clean energy projects in blue states, and delayed efforts to eliminate chemicals that are hundreds or thousands of times better at trapping heat than carbon dioxide. How is the administration justifying this? Trump officials argue that climate rules burden the fossil fuel industry, hurt the economy, and increase what Americans pay for electricity. In truth, the EPA’s own assessments recently found that repealing power-plant pollution standards will do little to lower utility bills or save the coal industry, which has been in decline for decades. But it would be a major favor to the GOP’s fossil fuel benefactors, saving the industry billions. The economic impacts for the rest of us will probably be imperceptible, particularly when compared to the damage Trump’s moves threaten to do to the environment and to public health. Surely there’s something that people worried about climate change can do about these new rules? Democratic-led states and environmental organizations will sue over these rules, as they have over the others. Those challenges could take years to resolve. But the Supreme Court seems likely to hear at least some of them, and its conservative majority has shown a blasé attitude toward climate change. In 2016, Chief Justice John Roberts led a behind-the-scenes effort to block the Clean Power Plan, an Obama initiative to slash emissions, in part citing “irreparable harm” to energy companies. In 2022, the court narrowed the EPA’s ability to regulate power plant emissions, forcing Joe Biden to adopt more-piecemeal measures. That doesn’t sound promising. So what would it mean if these rules took effect? Think of the fight against climate change like a tug-of-war, with emissions on one end of the rope and efforts to mitigate them on the other. Trump’s moves would tilt the playing field toward emissions. This means not only larger and faster changes to the climate but also an increase in other health effects due to the pollution that fossil-fuel-burning power plants spew, which can negatively affect anyone with lungs. The Biden administration estimated that its power plant rules would’ve prevented up to 1,200 premature deaths and 360,000 asthma attacks in 2035 alone. Some Democratic-led states have stepped into the breach, pursuing tougher rules of their own and even trying to force fossil fuel companies to pay for damage from wildfires, floods, and other climate-related disasters. Still, states probably can’t balance out a president who’s doing everything in his power to spur climate change along. And time is of the essence. Pondering the potential heat death of humanity is a bummer of a way to start the week, I know. To get your mind off it for the rest of the night, my colleagues suggest: TV worth rewatching: What is it about the HBO miniseries Band of Brothers that keeps viewers coming back for more, 25 years after it originally aired? Slate’s Rebecca Onion investigates why fans are so attached to the show. This game show tradition was Alex Trebek’s favorite: What is Jeopardy!’s annual Teen Tournament? Claire McNear, America’s preeminent Jeopardy! reporter, did a deep dive into the beloved event—which disappeared after Trebek’s death—and got some news about its future. Some snack science: Consumer Reports recently published a scary-sounding investigation on chemicals present in common kids’ snacks. Epidemiologist Gideon Meyerowitz-Katz breaks down the science and concludes that the results are actually pretty reassuring. A three-ingredient treat: If you’d rather nosh on a less processed snack, Noah Lederman recommends a super-fresh dish that’s perfect for late summer: a heavenly concoction he’s dubbed Fish Crudo With Melon and Tomato Water. OK, that’s all from me. Happy munching, and see you tomorrow! Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. If you value our work, please disable your ad blocker. Low-quality casino sites conceal highly dangerous threat actors
2026-09-15

Low-quality casino sites conceal highly dangerous threat actors

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Security firm Infoblox shines light on malicious infrastructure lurking beneath illegal gambling sites If your employees are visiting Chinese-language gambling or adult sites, they may not just be wasting time and money, but potentially encountering serious malware hidden behind domains that look like mostly harmless entertainment at first glance. A report from Infoblox urges the security community to pay closer attention to these websites, because some double as command-and-control (C2) infrastructure for espionage and malware distribution. Zach Edwards, staff threat researcher at Infoblox, suggests security researchers and the media have ignored these sites because the story is complicated and confusing. Infoblox says it tracks about 1.7 million Chinese-language casino websites that facilitate illegal gambling. These support North Korean money laundering and tax avoidance, among other dubious activities. And these casino sites can be difficult to distinguish from one another. They tend to use variations of common templates in terms of design and function. Many operate like a legal casino would, just relying on the advantage of house odds to profit. While these sites provide illegal gambling and adult entertainment for online visitors from China and Asia, some rely on US cloud providers for computing infrastructure. "Major US hosting companies (Amazon, Microsoft, Cloudflare, and Google) continue to host infrastructure associated with these domains," the Infoblox report explains. "One likely explanation is account theft at those providers, a practice documented previously as 'infrastructure laundering.'" That refers to hosting companies like Funnull that have reportedly rented IP addresses from Amazon Web Services and Microsoft and made those resources available to clients carrying out illegal activities. According to a July 2026 report from the UN Office on Drugs and Crime (UNODC), disparate crime syndicates increasingly use common infrastructure for cybercrime, while online scams resulted in estimated losses of between $88.3 billion and $114.1 billion in 2025 across East Asia, Southeast Asia, Australia, and New Zealand. A subset of casino sites offer scam gambling, or "scambling." Visitors place bets but can't get their money out if they win. And then there's a subset of sites used by China-aligned threat groups. "China-aligned APT groups have been running the PeckBirdy framework since 2023, hiding their malware C2 domains inside low-quality Chinese-language casino websites," Infoblox said. PeckBirdy, as noted by Trend Micro researchers in January, is a script-based framework that attackers can load through compromised websites. In one campaign, attackers injected scripts into gambling sites that loaded PeckBirdy and displayed fake software update pages designed to entice victims to download malware. The problem is that each of these three types of sites, though they change frequently, looks similar. Infoblox notes that just over 3 percent of its enterprise customers resolved at least one PeckBirdy C2 domain. "The most important thing for defenders to do is stop ignoring casino domains," Infoblox argues. "An alert on a Chinese-language casino or adult domain that gets closed as an employee browsing violation is precisely the outcome the PeckBirdy operators are counting on. The decoy works because the dismissal is reasonable – these domains genuinely are, most of the time, exactly what they appear to be." Security analysts who review suspicious network contacts are advised to check whether these casino domains include malicious payloads before closing the review ticket. ® 'Jev' doesn't chat. Higher-enriched uranium for datacenters has DoE all aglow
2026-09-15

Higher-enriched uranium for datacenters has DoE all aglow

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
HALEU is still being produced at a snail's pace; Nusano says its federal backing could speed things up – eventually The US Department of Energy has selected a new participant for a program aimed at expanding the domestic supply of fuel for next-generation nuclear reactors. However, we shouldn't expect that to mean the US' ever-growing fleet of datacenters will be powered by low-carbon nuclear energy any time soon. Utah-based Nusano announced on Monday that the DoE had selected it for its Nuclear Energy Launch Pad Program designed to help companies commercialize their nuclear power concepts. In this case, Nusano is working on a new method of creating high-assay low-enriched uranium, or HALEU, fuel that’s required for most next-generation nuclear reactors, like small modular reactors (SMRs) and molten salt reactors. HALEU is defined as uranium enay, low-riched to between 5 and 20 weight percent of U-235, the primary fissile isotope that nuclear reactors use to generate power. Traditional nuclear reactors use low-enriched uranium of under five percent. The higher enrichment is needed for smaller, more efficient reactor designs that are currently under development. Several advanced reactor demonstrations in the US have reached zero-power criticality, but none is yet operating commercially. The US has been without a steady commercial-scale supply of HALEU for years, with Russia historically the main commercial supplier and China also possessing HALEU enrichment capability. As of 2026, there’s only a single company in the US actively producing HALEU, and it’s not Nusano. The DoE has signed agreements with several others to produce more HALEU and related fuel products, but those firms are still establishing their facilities, and production remains minuscule. Centrus, the only American company producing HALEU, has generated less than two metric tons since it signed a contract with the government in 2019. Nusano expects to blow Centrus’ meager fuel generation out of the water with its HALEU enrichment design. That said, the company’s design hasn’t actually been realized yet. Nusano didn’t answer a question about whether it had built a functional demonstration HALEU enrichment unit, and when asked whether it had produced any HALEU, the company said that prototype development is still under way. “Nusano expects each unit will produce 5.9 metric tons (MT) of HALEU per year, with the first unit scheduled to be operational in 2031,” Nusano comms boss Scott Larrivee told The Register in an email. “Nusano’s Launch Pad application includes a well-developed, practical plan to move from proof-of-concept to commercial implementation.” As for what that’ll look like, Nusano describes a method that’s completely different from other forms of HALEU production. Whereas other HALEU refiners use gas centrifuges to produce HALEU, spinning uranium hexafluoride gas at high speeds to increase U-235 concentration, Nusano’s method ditches gas entirely. “The method is complex, costly, and potentially hazardous,” Larrivee explained. “If released, uranium hexafluoride reacts with moisture in air or human tissue to form highly toxic, corrosive hydrogen fluoride and uranyl fluoride.” Those gases, Larrivee said, can cause potentially fatal injuries like chemical burns and lung and kidney damage. Nusano is proposing a method that uses uranium feedstock that’s converted to metal prior to enrichment. Unspecified “proprietary separation techniques” are then used to enrich the metal and separate out U-235. The HALEU output of Nusano’s design is metal. “The Nusano process is metal in, metal out. No deconversion. No gaseous fluorine chemistry anywhere,” Larrivee added. Nusano’s current design only requires 1,200 square feet per HALEU unit, and the company anticipates its production cost will be lower than those of gas-centrifuge enrichment. That said, a lot of Nusano units will be needed to meet expected American demand. According to the DoE, HALEU demand could reach 50 metric tons per year by 2035, and Nusano is only expecting to get its first 5.9 MT/year unit up and running by 2031. It’s also not clear if Nusano’s current promises will still be the same if and when its HALEU units are up and running. In June 2025, the company said it had achieved a breakthrough that would allow a single HALEU unit to produce 50 metric tons annually and enable it to scale production to 350 metric tons per year by 2029. Search the company website for that announcement today, and it’s gone. We were only able to see it by pulling up an archived copy. Larrivee said that announcement was out of date, developed under prior company leadership, and that the numbers in the latest release were reflective of current development plans. A number of datacenter operators have promised to embrace SMRs and other next-gen nuclear when the technology is available. With HALEU output still low and commercial-scale production years away, it's unclear when those promises will be realized. Meanwhile, the Trump administration's Environmental Protection Agency has just scrapped Biden-era emissions regulations for coal and gas power plants, which it said means coal generation in the US could increase by more than ten times current levels. In other words, next-gen nuclear is, as ever, still on the horizon while bit barns crank up the fossil fuel turbines, now with even less regard to environmental impacts. ® 'Jev' doesn't chat. The Boox Palma 3 gets stylus support and a sleek redesign

The Verge | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
﻿The next version of Boox’s smartphone-sized e-reader ships with Android 16. ﻿The next version of Boox’s smartphone-sized e-reader ships with Android 16. If you buy something from a Verge link, Vox Media may earn a commission. See our ethics statement. Nearly two years after the last update to Boox’s smartphone-sized black-and-white e-reader, the company announced the Palma 3 with a sleek redesign, new functionality, and a handful of other small updates that may justify its significant price bump depending on how you plan to use it. While the Palma 2 launched in 2024 for $279.99, the Palma 3 will be $339.99 and is expected to be “available soon.” The most obvious upgrade for the Palma 3 is the hardware itself. It still features the same 6.13-inch, 300ppi E Ink Carta screen as its predecessor (which is now quite a few years old), but it’s wrapped in a new anodized aluminum frame with a paperlike fiberglass backplate. The Palma 3 looks like a significant upgrade to the Palma line, even if under the hood you’ll find more of the same. The pocketable e-reader is powered by a Qualcomm Q-6690 octa-core processor with 6GB of RAM and 128GB of storage expandable with a microSD card. It also includes a 3950mAh rechargeable battery, a 16-megapixel camera on the back, and a fingerprint reader in the power button. The original Palma and the Palma 2 came with Android 11 and Android 13, respectively, but the new Palma 3 ships with Android 16, which is just one generation behind the latest version of the OS. That should help it feel like a more modern mobile device. And like the color screen Palma 2 Pro, the Palma 3 supports the Boox InkSense Plus active stylus so you can use the e-reader as a digital notepad with your preferred note-taking app. But it’s not included. The stylus is an optional upgrade that will add another $46 to the cost of the Palma 3, pushing it closer to the price of premium e-notes like the Remarkable Paper Pure. The EOS R8 Mark II is Canon’s lightest full-frame camera with stabilization

The EOS R8 Mark II is Canon’s lightest full-frame camera with stabilization

The Verge | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
﻿The 24.2-megapixel camera features a retro redesign inspired by Canon’s old film cameras. ﻿The 24.2-megapixel camera features a retro redesign inspired by Canon’s old film cameras. If you buy something from a Verge link, Vox Media may earn a commission. See our ethics statement. Canon announced the second-generation of its EOS R8 with a new retro-inspired redesign and the addition of in-body stabilization. That feature was one of the most notable omissions from the original R8 that launched in 2023 as a smaller, cheaper, and more accessible alternative to the Canon R6 Mark II for photographers wanting a larger full-frame shooter. While the new EOS R8 Mark II is slightly heavier than its predecessor at 1.2 pounds thanks to the additional stabilization functionality, it’s lighter and cheaper than last year’s EOS R6 Mark III. The Canon EOS R8 Mark II is expected to be available sometime in late October for $1,899 for the body only, or $2,099 bundled with a Canon 24-50mm stabilized lens. That’s more expensive than the original R8 that launched at $1,499, but more accessible than the $2,799 EOS R6 Mark III thanks to some lower specs and omitted features. Like its predecessor, the R8 Mark II features the same 24.2-megapixel full-frame CMOS sensor as the R6 Mark II, while the R6 Mark III got a bump to a 32.5-megapixel sensor. The new R8 Mark II still exclusively uses an electronic shutter instead of an additional mechanical one, with high-speed captures maxing out at 40fps again. To ensure you’re not too late to a critical moment, a pre-continuous shooting mode captures up to an additional 20 shots before you press the shutter button. The new five-axis in-body stabilization offers up to 7.5 stops of shake correction that can be expanded by pairing the camera with a lens featuring its own stabilization. The R8 Mark II also features Canon’s Dual Pixel CMOS AF II system with what the company describes as “more tenacious tracking” than what the R8 offered. The autofocus system can also recognize animals, vehicles, and people, with the option to register up to 10 different people by their faces and rank their priority. Video capabilities max out at 4K/60fps, but frame rates can be boosted to up to 180fps by dropping the resolution to 1080p. As Canon is targeting the R8 Mark II as a potential full-frame option for amateur photographers, the camera includes 16 different color filters, an easy to way to increase or decrease depth of blur that handles settings like f-stop and shutter speed automatically, and creative bracketing that takes three photos with different brightness and color tones. Following other companies drawing inspiration from older film cameras for the design of new digital shooters, the R8 Mark II eschews the curves of the original R8 for a more boxy and angular body. Canon credits the nearly 40-year-old EOS 650 and its pentaprism housing on top as the inspiration for the R8 Mark II. Other hardware upgrades include a new multi-controller joystick above the camera’s screen, which makes it easier to select focal points, and its single SD card slot repositioned on the side of the camera so it’s still accessible when using a tripod. Canon will also sell a matching $99.99 accessory that extends the R8 Mark II’s grip, making it easier to hold when using larger lenses. In October Canon is also releasing a free new Windows app called Dual Pixel 3D Converter that leverages additional info captured by Canon’s dual-pixel autofocus system to generate 3D imagery from the 2D photos the camera takes. You can use the app to create a 3D photo with a background you can move around, a 3D model with the background completely removed, or short 3D videos demonstrating an animated perspective adjustment of close-up subjects. AI and data centers are incredibly unpopular in every poll
2026-09-15

AI and data centers are incredibly unpopular in every poll

The Verge | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
61 percent of likely voters said they oppose the construction of AI data centers, but neither major party has much of an edge. 61 percent of likely voters said they oppose the construction of AI data centers, but neither major party has much of an edge. Poll data released Tuesday by the New York Times and Siena University confirms what we’ve already been seeing, and what politicians are responding to — AI and data centers are incredibly unpopular. Asked if they support or oppose the construction of data centers to power AI tech, 61 percent of the 1,503 likely voters surveyed in early September (ahead of the recent AI safety slowdown discussion) said they were opposed, with only 14 percent saying they strongly support. The split was about even among 2024 Donald Trump voters, at 49 percent support to 45 percent oppose, while people who had voted for Kamala Harris or who didn’t vote leaned oppose, at 74 percent and 67 percent, respectively. 56 percent of voters surveyed who said they were opposed to data centers said they supported limits, while 38 percent instead preferred a total ban. The list of reasons for opposition is also revealing, with the environment/water usage most common at 32 percent, impact on local communities at 21 percent, general distrust or dislike of AI at 18 percent, followed by regulation concerns and the economy at 10 and 9 percent. An NBC News poll in March showed AI polling lower than ICE, and a Gallup poll in May revealed seven in ten Americans opposed local construction of AI data centers. Still, the data released today ranked AI and data centers low on the list of issues that voters saw as most important in deciding their midterm vote. Across the board, AI and data centers registered at less than one percent for most demographics, with exceptions for younger voters like the 18-29 group, where three percent picked that one instead of the most popular responses like the economy, foreign policy, inflation, and opposition to Trump/Republicans. Voters in either party still overwhelmingly picked their own party as the one they trusted to handle the issue, while overall the 554 respondents had 40 percent favoring Democrats, 42 percent favoring Republicans, and 18 percent saying they didn’t know or refused. The Verge
What if social media isn’t hurting kids?
2026-09-15

What if social media isn’t hurting kids?

The Verge | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
A new book says modern schooling, not phones, are behind youth mental health declines. Will anyone listen? A new book says modern schooling, not phones, are behind youth mental health declines. Will anyone listen? Two years ago, social psychologist Jonathan Haidt released his New York Times bestseller The Anxious Generation. The book posits that the rise of social media and smartphone use is largely to blame for the prominent decline in teen mental health since 2010. It quickly became a central rallying cry of the social media backlash. Haidt has been credited with helping inspire Australia’s nationwide teen social media ban. He has joined forces with bereaved parents who are fighting tech companies in court. His name comes up unprompted in conversations with people in power who say his book opened their eyes to an online crisis. Haidt’s theory has received regular critiques from recognized academics. Candice Odgers, a leading adolescent mental health researcher, wrote a scathing review of Haidt’s analysis in Nature that opened by conceding, “this book is going to sell a lot of copies, because Jonathan Haidt is telling a scary story about children’s development that many parents are primed to believe.” Now, in the wake of that highly accurate prediction, a former colleague of Haidt’s seeks to not only take down his theory, but also offer an alternative. The biggest question may not be whether he’s correct — but whether it’s too late to change course if he is. In his new book Restoring Childhood, psychology researcher Peter Gray argues that teens’ mental health declined thanks to decades of steadily reduced autonomy, and after a brief period of reprieve, fell again in the 2010s not — as Haidt argues — because of phones and social media, but because of rigid schooling methods linked to the Common Core curriculum and government pressure to improve children’s performance. And far from being damaging, Gray also argues the adoption of home internet in the 1990s and 2000s actually drove a rebound in teens’ mental health by restoring freedom and connection lost in earlier decades. He fears social media bans aren’t just — as Odgers has argued — a distraction from real solutions, but a new harm in themselves. “You take a smartphone away from your kids and that study suggests you’ll make them less happy” In an interview on The Vergecast, Gray told me that Haidt’s book “completely ignored” dozens of meta-analyses and other research that’s found “not even a meaningful correlation between the amount of time kids are on social media or on smartphones and their mental health.” Where there is some connection, Gray adds, studies suggest that teens may use social media to cope with their depression or anxiety. He references a study in Florida that found 11-to-13-year-olds who owned smartphones were more likely to spend real-world time with friends. “You take a smartphone away from your kids and that study suggests you’ll make them less happy and they’ll have less friends,” he says. Gray says that in studies through the years, kids consistently identify school pressure, not social media, as their top source of anxiety. “That doesn’t mean it’s the primary source for everybody, but when you’re looking at the population at large, every single study that I’ve been able to find where kids are asked, ‘What’s the primary source of your anxiety or your stress or your depression?’, in every single one of them, school pressure comes out on top,” Gray tells me. “Nothing else comes close.” Gray and Haidt both believe that since at least the 1980s, increased restrictions on kids’ independence and unstructured play have damaged their resilience. While it used to be common for kids to play together unsupervised, that practice has diminished in part due to the rise of “stranger danger” narratives fostered by rare, but high-profile, crimes. In 2017 the pair helped cofound an organization called Let Grow, which advocates for policies that make it easier for parents to let their children play freely, like ensuring they can’t be considered neglectful for letting kids outside by themselves. But the two have split sharply over the impact of the internet. Gray’s book is intended as a deliberate rebuttal to Haidt’s — after The Anxious Generation’s release, Gray resigned from the board of Let Grow to avoid conflict within the organization. Haidt did not respond to a request for comment from The Verge, but he told The Atlantic’s Kaitlyn Tiffany in a July article that the 1990s and early 2000s dip in teen suicide rates may be credited to a phase-out of leaded gasoline, since lead exposure has been linked to mental health issues. He also told Tiffany that Gray relied too heavily on a minority dissenting group of researchers. Tiffany writes that many of the more than a dozen tech and child development researchers she spoke to “expressed concern that Haidt overstates the strength of correlational findings and suggests causation where it hasn’t been proved.” Gray accuses Haidt of stirring a moral panic over technology that isn’t, on the whole, harmful. Looking across research, he says, Common Core is the clearest explanation for the sudden decline in teen mental health. And at the population level, the impact of social media “balances out. Some kids are doing better, some kids are doing worse because they’re on social media.” “We need to stop treating kids as if they’re idiots” Gray says he’s not opposed to all guardrails, including school phone bans and the concept of age verification for accessing pornography. But he questions the effectiveness of bans on social media, knowing kids are likely to get around them, and is hesitant to restrict platforms that let kids express themselves — suggesting instead that parents focus on teaching their children to navigate them. “We need to stop treating kids as if they’re idiots,” he told me. “We need to start understanding that kids are pretty good at knowing what’s good for them and not good for them and they can benefit from our help in learning how to do these things.” But increasingly, parents of younger teens may not have that choice. Haidt’s theory holds undisputed dominance in many circles, including among politicians. Since Australia’s social media ban for teens, the UK and European Union are among those considering their own bans or platform limits. In the US, states like California and New York have enacted limits on certain social media features, and a federal proposal would restrict algorithmic feeds for kids under 17. Even among people who find Gray’s arguments about Common Core convincing, some might still see taking kids offline as a harmless precaution. With so much momentum behind the backlash against phones and social media, can Gray’s book still make a difference? “I certainly hope it’s not too late,” he says. He points out that some moral panics have petered out. In the 1990s and 2000s, for instance, many people worried that video games like Mortal Kombat and Doom were making kids more violent, connecting their rise to that of school shootings. Eventually, research showed many mass shooters didn’t play video games after all. Attempts at regulation — including a California ban on the sale of violent games to children — were largely overruled. And many kids who did play them grew up to become those in charge. “The science won out eventually,” Gray says. “So it’s not me that’s going to win out. It’s the science that’s going to win out.” A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Sign in to see your notifications or create an account to join the conversation.

Kia’s electric van lineup is getting more interesting with reveal of PV7
